The argument is not that everyone must use every tool or adopt every feature. It is that ignorance is a poor substitute for deliberate choice. A thoughtful organization can decide not to use AI for a particular purpose after assessing privacy, safety, fairness, or reliability, while still maintaining enough knowledge to understand the technology and its effects on the surrounding environment.
Study instruction:
- "Write a policy for one setting that distinguishes learning about AI, approved use, restricted use, and justified non-use."
Key point:
- Opting out of awareness does not stop external change.
- Informed non-use is different from refusing to understand the technology.
Source links:
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- OECD: AI Principles
22. Q: When can deliberately not using AI be a responsible choice rather than avoidance?
A: Show answer
Non-use can be appropriate when a system cannot meet the required standard of accuracy, confidentiality, safety, fairness, explainability, or legal compliance. A task may require human judgment, direct contact, or professional responsibility that the available system cannot provide. An organization may also prohibit AI use with sensitive data or in decisions where the risks outweigh the benefits. The chapter criticizes opting out as a general strategy for avoiding change, not every carefully reasoned restriction. A responsible decision records the purpose, evidence, risk assessment, alternatives, affected people, and review date. It remains open to reconsideration if technology or safeguards change.
Study instruction:
- "Assess one proposed AI use and document whether adoption, restricted use, or non-use is justified by the task's risks and requirements."
Key point:
- Responsible non-use is evidence-based and specific to a use case.
- Avoidance is passive; governance is a deliberate decision with reasons and review.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
23. Q: Why does the chapter describe convenience-first delegation as a second dead end?
A: Show answer
Convenience-first delegation occurs when a person asks AI to think or decide mainly to reduce immediate effort, then accepts the output without examining it. This can look productive because tasks move quickly and more material is generated. Yet the apparent efficiency may conceal errors, weak reasoning, or a loss of the user's understanding. The chapter considers this more dangerous than simple reluctance because it can actively train people to bypass judgment while giving them a sense of progress. A better approach is to delegate suitable mechanical work while retaining responsibility for framing the problem, checking evidence, resolving ambiguity, and deciding what action is justified.
Study instruction:
- "Review a recent AI-assisted task and mark which steps were delegated, which were reviewed, and which decisions were genuinely understood by the human."
Key point:
- Output volume is not the same as value or understanding.
- Delegation should be bounded by task suitability and meaningful human review.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- OECD: AI Principles
24. Q: What is lost when a user lets AI summarize before doing any synthesis of their own?
A: Show answer
The user may lose the opportunity to notice patterns, contradictions, uncertainties, and relationships that emerge from first-hand engagement with the material. A summary is a compressed interpretation: it selects what appears important and may omit information that does not fit its framing. If the user has not formed an initial view, they may have little basis for detecting those omissions. The chapter therefore favors a sequence in which the person first considers the problem, identifies questions, or records an initial interpretation, and then uses AI to challenge and extend it. AI summaries can still be useful, but they should be compared with source material and the user's own reasoning.
Study instruction:
- "For one article or report, write an initial summary before asking AI for one; compare what each includes, omits, and emphasizes."
Key point:
- Independent synthesis gives the user a basis for evaluating an AI summary.
- AI summaries should be checked against the original evidence and the purpose of the task.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
25. Q: How can a user distinguish useful assistance from a conversation that is merely getting longer?
A: Show answer
A longer exchange is useful only when it improves the user's model of the problem, clarifies uncertainties, tests assumptions, or leads to a better decision. Repetition, tangents with no relevance, and increasingly elaborate but unverified suggestions can create activity without progress. The user should periodically state what has been learned, what remains uncertain, which claims have evidence, and what decision or next step is now possible. A good interaction can also reveal that the original question was wrong or incomplete. The chapter values sustained exploration, but thoughtful engagement is not unlimited conversation; it is a purposeful process with checkpoints and a human-defined objective.
Study instruction:
- "Set three checkpoints for an AI discussion: clarify the problem, test the leading explanation, and summarize verified conclusions and open questions."
Key point:
- Interaction quality should be judged by learning and decision value, not message count.
- Periodic synthesis prevents exploration from becoming unstructured activity.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
26. Q: What employment risks does the chapter associate with AI, and how should they be understood?
A: Show answer
The chapter warns that roles may change or disappear faster than workers and institutions can adapt, and that skills developed over many years may lose market value. This is a concern about the distribution and pace of transition, not a claim that every occupation will vanish. The International Labour Organization's 2025 global index found that about one in four jobs worldwide is potentially exposed to generative AI, while transformation rather than full replacement was assessed as the more likely overall outcome. Exposure means tasks may be affected; it is not a count of jobs already eliminated. Outcomes depend on task composition, adoption, management choices, regulation, worker bargaining power, and the creation of new work.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one occupation and divide its tasks into likely automation, augmentation, human-interaction, and accountability components."
Key point:
- Task exposure is not the same as job loss.
- The pace and fairness of transition depend on institutions, training, and choices by employers and policymakers.
Source links:
- ILO: Generative AI and Jobs, 2025
- OECD: AI Principles
27. Q: How should workers and employers respond to the possibility that AI will change job tasks?
A: Show answer
Workers can identify which parts of their roles involve routine information processing and which require domain judgment, relationships, physical presence, accountability, or contextual understanding. They can build AI literacy while strengthening skills that help them direct, evaluate, and improve automated work. Employers should involve workers in redesign, provide practical training, assess job-quality effects, and avoid treating short-term productivity as the only measure of success. Governments and social partners can support transitions through education, social dialogue, and appropriate social protection. The chapter's call for engagement is therefore not simply an individual productivity message; it also raises a collective responsibility to manage disruption fairly.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a skills-transition plan for one role, including current tasks, emerging tasks, training needs, and safeguards for affected workers."
Key point:
- Adaptation requires both individual learning and institutional support.
- A fair transition considers job quality, worker voice, and distribution of benefits.
Source links:
- ILO: Generative AI and Jobs, 2025
- OECD: AI Principles
28. Q: Why does the author describe the current period as a technological transition rather than a settled end state?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that AI capabilities, products, norms, and governance are evolving at the same time. Users are experimenting, organizations are redesigning workflows, and institutions are still developing rules and expectations. In such a period, both risks and opportunities may be unusually visible because practices have not stabilized. The author uses historical transitions to suggest that early confusion does not determine the final outcome. However, transition is not a guarantee of eventual improvement. Decisions about standards, infrastructure, access, accountability, education, and worker protection can shape which possibilities become real. The period should therefore be treated as open to influence rather than as a finished system.
Study instruction:
- "Create a transition map showing current capabilities, emerging practices, unresolved rules, affected groups, and decisions that could shape the next stage."
Key point:
- Technological transitions combine uncertainty, experimentation, and institutional change.
- The direction of change remains influenced by policy and human decisions.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
29. Q: How does the printing-press analogy help explain the chapter's view of AI?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents the printing press as a technology that expanded access to written material and literacy while also spreading misinformation, destabilizing authority, and contributing to conflict. The analogy illustrates that a major innovation can create benefits and harms simultaneously, and that its consequences depend on how people and institutions respond. It also warns against judging a technology only by its earliest disruptive effects. The analogy is not proof that AI will follow the same path or that every harm will eventually be outweighed by benefits. It is a prompt to examine the longer sequence of institutional adaptation, new capabilities, misuse, and corrective action.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one beneficial and one harmful consequence of the printing press described in the chapter, then compare the mechanisms with AI without assuming identical outcomes."
Key point:
- Historical analogies reveal patterns but do not establish a guaranteed forecast.
- New communication capabilities can expand knowledge and amplify manipulation at the same time.
30. Q: What does the chapter learn from the history of industrialization?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes industrialization as a transition that increased productive capacity and prosperity over time while also displacing workers, enabling exploitation, and concentrating economic power. The lesson is that technological productivity does not automatically produce fair or humane outcomes. Institutions, labor protections, education, public policy, and collective action influence how gains and costs are distributed. Applied to AI, this means that productivity improvements should be assessed alongside worker displacement, job quality, market concentration, and access to new opportunities. The analogy also cautions against assuming that because society eventually adapted to earlier technologies, present harms can be ignored or will resolve themselves without deliberate action.
Study instruction:
- "Compare industrialization and AI across productivity, displacement, concentration of power, new occupations, and the institutions needed to manage change."
Key point:
- Productivity gains can coexist with exploitation and unequal distribution.
- Institutional responses help determine whether technological change benefits a broad population.
Source links:
- ILO: Generative AI and Jobs, 2025
- OECD: AI Principles
31. Q: What does the chapter's comparison with the internet contribute to its argument?
A: Show answer
The internet connected people and information across the world, but it also fragmented attention, enabled new forms of manipulation, and created security and privacy vulnerabilities. The chapter uses this mixed legacy to show that connectivity alone does not guarantee informed or constructive communication. AI may similarly lower barriers to creation and access while increasing the scale of synthetic content, automated influence, and information overload. The comparison encourages readers to ask what new practices, safeguards, and institutions are needed rather than treating technical capability as an adequate measure of social progress. It also reminds readers that benefits and risks can grow together.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one internet-era harm and one benefit and identify what governance or user practices could be adapted to AI."
Key point:
- Greater access to information does not automatically improve information quality.
- Technology's social effects depend on incentives, safeguards, and user behavior.
Source links:
- NIST: Reducing Risks Posed by Synthetic Content
- OECD: AI Principles
32. Q: What does the author mean by saying earlier technological transitions became positive-sum?
A: Show answer
The author argues that the printing press, industrialization, and the internet ultimately expanded the total range of human capabilities and opportunities, even though their transitions involved serious harm. “Positive-sum” means that the overall possibilities or value available to society grew rather than remaining a fixed quantity divided among people. The chapter uses this historical interpretation to support optimism about AI. It should be read as the author's broad thesis, not a mathematical proof that every person benefited or that every harm was repaired. Gains can be uneven, costs can persist, and some communities can lose even while aggregate capacity grows. The claim is most useful as a reason to shape change, not as permission to minimize damage.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate the positive-sum claim by listing aggregate gains, groups that bore costs, harms that persisted, and policies that affected distribution."
Key point:
- The chapter's positive-sum argument is an interpretation of long-term history, not a guarantee.
- Aggregate progress does not ensure that benefits and burdens are shared fairly.
Source links:
- OECD: AI Principles
- ILO: Generative AI and Jobs, 2025
33. Q: Why does the chapter reject the idea that progress happens automatically?
A: Show answer
The author says that better outcomes arise because people repeatedly work to reduce suffering, build fairer systems, and solve problems rather than exploit disruption. This places human agency, institutional effort, and public participation at the center of technological change. Innovation may create new capabilities, but it does not decide how they are distributed, which harms are tolerated, or what standards govern their use. Governments, businesses, workers, researchers, educators, and civil society all influence the direction of change. The chapter's optimism is therefore active: people must participate in building safeguards and opportunities. Waiting for technology or markets to produce fair results is not a substitute for governance.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the actors who could influence one AI-related problem and specify the decision or action each actor can realistically take."
Key point:
- Progress requires deliberate human and institutional effort.
- Technology creates options; governance and collective action influence the outcomes.
Source links:
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- OECD: AI Principles
34. Q: What are first-order effects of a technology, and why are they easier to identify?
A: Show answer
First-order effects are the direct and relatively immediate changes produced when a technology is introduced. For a lawn mower, the chapter identifies cheaper and less labor-intensive grass cutting as the obvious first-order effect. These effects are often easier to forecast because they are close to the technology's intended function. They can be measured through immediate changes in time, cost, capacity, or performance. However, a first-order assessment can miss how the technology changes constraints, incentives, infrastructure, and behavior elsewhere. The chapter argues that focusing only on the direct task can underestimate the broader transformation that follows once people begin using the new capability in different settings.
Study instruction:
- "For an AI tool, identify its direct function and measure its immediate effects on time, cost, quality, and labor."
Key point:
- First-order effects are close to the technology's intended task.
- Direct performance measures do not capture the full system impact.
35. Q: What are second-order and third-order effects, and how do they differ from direct effects?
A: Show answer
Second-order effects arise when the first change alters what people can do, how they organize, or which constraints they face. Third-order effects can emerge from those new activities and their interactions with other systems. They may appear in industries or institutions unrelated to the technology's original purpose. The chapter's lawn-mower example moves from cheaper grass cutting to more uniform playing fields, then to organized sports infrastructure and spectator activity. Such chains are difficult to forecast because each stage creates conditions for further adaptation. A systems analysis therefore asks not only what the tool does, but what becomes feasible, who responds, and which new dependencies or markets may emerge.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a three-stage causal chain for one AI capability, separating direct effects from later changes in behavior, infrastructure, and institutions."
Key point:
- Later effects arise through changed constraints and human responses.
- Second- and third-order effects are often less predictable than immediate performance.
36. Q: How does Edward Budding's lawn-mower example illustrate the chapter's “Budding Effect”?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Edward Budding's 1830 grass-cutting machine as a technology whose immediate purpose was to reduce the cost and labor of maintaining lawns. The less obvious consequence was that uniform playing surfaces became easier to create and maintain. The chapter links this change to the development of organized sports grounds and spectator athletics. The example illustrates how removing a practical constraint can enable activities that were previously difficult or uneconomic. It does not show that one invention alone caused modern sport; many social, economic, cultural, and organizational developments were also involved. The important idea is that downstream consequences may emerge far from the original design problem.
Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct the chapter's lawn-mower chain and add at least two intervening conditions that would be needed for organized sport to grow."
Key point:
- A technology can enable activities beyond its original purpose.
- The causal chain includes social and institutional conditions, not only the invention itself.
37. Q: Should the “Budding Effect” be treated as a reliable law for predicting technological change?
A: Show answer
No. In the chapter, the Budding Effect is a way to describe how removing a constraint can enable unexpected downstream activity. It is a useful lens for asking better questions, but it does not provide a formula for predicting which consequences will appear or when. Many innovations do not generate the same kind of spillover, and some second-order effects are harmful, blocked, or captured by a small number of actors. A disciplined analysis should develop multiple scenarios, identify enabling conditions, and monitor emerging evidence rather than assume that a positive transformation will follow. The concept encourages openness to indirect effects while preserving uncertainty about their direction and distribution.
Study instruction:
- "Use the Budding Effect as a scenario prompt, then list conditions that could enable, block, or reverse the expected downstream outcome."
Key point:
- The concept is a thinking tool, not a deterministic forecasting rule.
- Unexpected effects can be beneficial, harmful, or unevenly distributed.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
38. Q: How could AI's energy demands produce second-order effects in the energy system?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes that rising AI electricity demand could accelerate investment in clean-energy infrastructure, nuclear research, grid modernization, and more efficient computing. These are possible responses to a new demand, not guaranteed outcomes. The International Energy Agency's analysis supports examining both sides of the energy-AI relationship: data centres require electricity, while AI may also help optimize energy systems and industrial processes. Whether investment improves affordability or sustainability depends on the energy mix, grid constraints, siting, financing, regulation, and whether efficiency gains outpace growth in total use. A sound analysis therefore tracks both the burden created by AI and the wider energy improvements that may result.
Study instruction:
- "Build two scenarios: one in which AI demand accelerates cleaner infrastructure and another in which fossil generation and local grid stress increase."
Key point:
- AI can be both a new source of electricity demand and a tool for energy-system improvement.
- The net result depends on infrastructure, policy, efficiency, and total demand.
Source links:
- International Energy Agency: Energy and AI
- IEA: Energy Demand from AI
39. Q: Why does the chapter describe AI-enabled access to expertise as a potentially transformative second-order effect?
A: Show answer
The chapter imagines expert-level synthesis becoming available at very low marginal cost to people outside wealthy or highly specialized institutions. It gives examples such as a rural doctor in Malawi gaining access to diagnostic knowledge and a researcher in Nairobi surveying published work. The proposed transformation is not merely faster text generation; it is a possible reduction in barriers to expertise, research, legal information, agricultural science, engineering, and education. If more people can investigate difficult problems, new combinations of local knowledge and broader information may emerge. The examples are illustrative possibilities, not evidence that AI can replace local professionals or deliver equal-quality services everywhere.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one underserved setting and map which expertise barriers AI might reduce and which barriers would remain."
Key point:
- The largest benefit may come from removing access constraints rather than automating a single task.
- Illustrative access does not prove professional equivalence or reliable outcomes.
Source links:
- OECD: AI Principles
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
40. Q: Why does access to AI expertise not automatically create equal outcomes?
A: Show answer
People may have access to a tool but lack reliable connectivity, suitable devices, relevant language support, high-quality data, domain knowledge, institutional authority, or the ability to act on its advice. A rural clinician still needs patient examination, local protocols, diagnostic tests, and professional accountability. A researcher still needs methods, primary sources, peer review, and resources to validate findings. Outputs may also reflect biases or gaps in training data. Equitable benefit therefore requires infrastructure, education, local adaptation, safeguards, and participation by affected communities. The chapter's examples are most persuasive when read as possibilities that require complementary systems, not as claims that access alone solves structural inequality.
Study instruction:
- "For one community, list the infrastructure, skills, local knowledge, safeguards, and decision rights needed to turn AI access into a real benefit."
Key point:
- Access is only one condition for effective use.
- Equity depends on complementary resources, local relevance, and accountability.
Source links:
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- OECD: AI Principles
41. Q: What does the chapter mean by saying that the biggest consequences of AI may not be obvious today?
A: Show answer
The author argues that the immediate abilities to generate text, images, or code are only the visible first-order capabilities. More consequential changes may occur when those abilities remove friction in research, coordination, learning, decision-making, or access to specialized knowledge. These effects may appear in settings that were not the original target of the technology. They are difficult to predict because people adapt, combine tools with existing institutions, and create new uses after constraints disappear. The chapter's claim should encourage scenario exploration, not confident predictions. It also means that AI evaluation should look beyond task speed to changes in opportunity, power, dependence, and social organization.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one constraint in your sector and describe two new activities that might become possible if AI reduced that constraint."
Key point:
- Indirect effects can matter more than the tool's immediate function.
- Scenario thinking is more appropriate than pretending to know every downstream result.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
42. Q: What is the difference between a possibility and a prediction in the chapter's discussion of AI's future?
A: Show answer
A possibility describes an outcome that could occur if certain conditions are met. A prediction asserts that an outcome is expected, often with an implied judgment about likelihood or timing. The chapter explicitly presents examples such as cleaner energy infrastructure and wider access to expertise as possibilities rather than guaranteed forecasts. This distinction matters because technological systems involve uncertainty about adoption, economics, regulation, infrastructure, and human behavior. Responsible communication should state the assumptions behind a scenario, identify evidence that would strengthen or weaken it, and avoid presenting an attractive possibility as an inevitable result. Optimism is more credible when uncertainty remains visible.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite three optimistic claims about AI as conditional scenarios, naming the assumptions and indicators that would need to hold."
Key point:
- A plausible future is not automatically a forecast.
- Good scenario analysis states conditions, uncertainty, and evidence that could change the view.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
43. Q: Why does the author argue that thoughtful engagement can outperform raw adoption in the early stages of a technological transition?
A: Show answer
The chapter suggests that early adopters often gain access to new capabilities before good practices become widespread. People who experiment carefully, supply context, question weak outputs, and connect AI to real problems can create more value than people who merely use a tool because it is fashionable. The advantage comes from quality of engagement rather than speed of adoption. This is not a permanent guarantee: practices, products, and competition change, and early experimentation can also create risks. The practical lesson is to learn deliberately, build judgment, and develop responsible workflows while the technology and its norms are still developing.
Study instruction:
- "Compare two uses of the same AI tool: a quick generic request and a context-rich iterative investigation; record the difference in usefulness and verification effort."
Key point:
- Careful engagement can create value beyond simply acquiring a tool.
- Early advantage depends on learning and judgment, not novelty alone.
Source links:
- OECD: AI Principles
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
44. Q: How can two people using the same AI tool obtain very different results?
A: Show answer
The chapter's example contrasts a person who asks a quick question and accepts a generic answer with another who provides context, asks follow-up questions, challenges shallow responses, and develops the problem over time. The tool may be identical, but the interaction differs in the information supplied, the quality of questions, the user's willingness to examine assumptions, and the effort spent verifying the result. Domain knowledge also matters because a knowledgeable user can recognize missing information and ask more discriminating questions. Different results do not prove that prompt wording alone controls model capability; they show that the user and the interaction are part of the overall system.
Study instruction:
- "Run the same work task through a one-shot prompt and a structured dialogue, then compare accuracy, relevance, assumptions, and actionability."
Key point:
- The user contributes context, evaluation, and domain judgment to the outcome.
- Tool capability alone does not determine the quality of the work.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
45. Q: Why does the chapter argue that the quality of engagement matters more than raw adoption?
A: Show answer
Raw adoption measures whether a tool is being used, but not whether it improves the work or creates new risks. Quality of engagement considers whether the user understands the task, supplies relevant context, asks useful questions, evaluates evidence, and remains accountable for decisions. A team can deploy AI widely and still produce generic, inaccurate, or unsafe outputs if it lacks these practices. Conversely, a limited pilot can create substantial value if it addresses a genuine problem and includes good review. Organizations should therefore measure outcomes such as accuracy, time saved after review, error rates, user learning, accessibility, and unintended effects rather than counting licenses or prompts.
Study instruction:
- "Design a small AI pilot evaluation with baseline performance, quality measures, review time, risk indicators, and a decision rule for continuation."
Key point:
- Adoption metrics measure activity, not value.
- Evaluation should include quality, safety, learning, and downstream consequences.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- OECD: AI Principles
46. Q: What does the chapter suggest about prompt engineering compared with structuring one's thinking?
A: Show answer
The author argues that technical mastery of elaborate prompt formulas is less important than learning how to structure a problem so insight can emerge. A useful interaction begins with a clear purpose, relevant context, unresolved questions, and a willingness to refine the problem. The user can ask AI to identify missing information, compare interpretations, or challenge assumptions. This approach is broader than memorizing prompt tricks because it depends on judgment and an understanding of the subject. Prompt design still matters, especially for precise tasks, but it should serve the thinking process rather than become a substitute for it.
Study instruction:
- "Take a vague request and redesign it as a structured inquiry with context, objective, constraints, unknowns, and a request for critical questions."
Key point:
- Prompt technique is useful when it serves a well-framed problem.
- The deeper skill is organizing inquiry and evaluating the response.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
47. Q: Why does the author emphasize curiosity and patience rather than enthusiasm or fear?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes thoughtful engagement as a calm willingness to explore, learn, and allow understanding to develop before reaching conclusions. Enthusiasm can encourage uncritical adoption, while fear can lead to disengagement or reflexive rejection. Curiosity keeps a person open to useful capabilities and unexpected effects; patience creates room to ask follow-up questions and examine uncertainty. This stance does not mean being neutral about harm. The author explicitly says that environmental, intellectual-property, trust, cognitive, and employment concerns remain real. Curiosity and patience are intended to support better judgment, not to excuse irresponsible deployment.
Study instruction:
- "Reflect on a recent AI decision and identify whether enthusiasm, fear, or deliberate curiosity influenced it; record what evidence would improve the next decision."
Key point:
- Curiosity supports learning without requiring uncritical enthusiasm.
- Patience allows evidence and understanding to develop before action.
Source links:
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- OECD: AI Principles
48. Q: How can an individual use AI in a way that develops rather than bypasses judgment?
A: Show answer
Begin by stating the problem and recording an initial view before requesting a solution. Provide the relevant context and constraints, ask the system to identify missing information, and explore more than one explanation. Challenge claims that sound confident but lack evidence. Check important facts against reliable sources and ask what could make the proposed answer wrong. Then summarize the reasoning independently and decide what action is appropriate. For consequential health, legal, financial, employment, or safety matters, use qualified professionals and formal processes where required. The chapter's principle is not that humans must perform every task unaided, but that they should remain active learners and accountable decision-makers.
Study instruction:
- "Apply a five-step routine to one real task: attempt, contextualize, question, verify, and explain the decision in your own words."
Key point:
- Human judgment is strengthened by active participation and verification.
- AI assistance should not replace professional responsibility or required review.
Source links:
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
49. Q: What should a user do before asking AI to solve a complex problem?
A: Show answer
The user should define the decision or outcome, describe the situation, state relevant constraints, and identify what is already known and uncertain. A short initial attempt helps reveal assumptions and gives the user a basis for evaluating the response. The user should also identify sensitive information that must not be shared, any required professional or organizational rules, and what evidence would count as a reliable answer. A good opening does not need to be perfectly written. It needs to provide enough context for useful inquiry and make clear where the AI system's role ends. The chapter favors a structured conversation over a vague command for a finished product.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a problem brief containing objective, background, constraints, stakeholders, known facts, uncertainties, sensitive data, and success criteria."
Key point:
- A clear problem frame improves relevance and exposes missing context.
- Privacy, policy, and evidence requirements should be considered before input is shared.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
50. Q: How should users move from exploratory AI dialogue to a reliable decision?
A: Show answer
Exploration should end with a synthesis that separates verified facts, plausible interpretations, unresolved questions, and recommendations. The user should compare alternatives against the original objective and constraints, check important claims using primary or authoritative sources, and identify risks or affected stakeholders. The final decision should name the responsible person, the evidence relied upon, and any conditions or monitoring required. If uncertainty remains material, the appropriate result may be further investigation or escalation rather than a premature answer. This prevents a long dialogue from being mistaken for proof and preserves a clear line between AI-generated suggestions and human-authorized action.
Study instruction:
- "Create a decision note from an AI discussion with sections for verified evidence, assumptions, options, risks, owner, and next review date."
Key point:
- Exploration is not complete until claims are checked and decisions are owned.
- A documented synthesis preserves uncertainty and accountability.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
51. Q: What governance principles can help organizations engage with AI responsibly?
A: Show answer
Organizations should define approved purposes, assess impacts before deployment, assign accountable owners, protect privacy and security, test performance, document limitations, and monitor outcomes throughout the system lifecycle. Staff need training on appropriate use, prohibited inputs, verification, escalation, and human review. High-impact applications require stronger safeguards and a clear route for challenge or correction. UNESCO emphasizes human rights, accountability, transparency, safety, sustainability, and human oversight. The OECD AI Principles similarly emphasize human-centred values, transparency, robustness, accountability, and preparing people for labour-market change. These principles do not replace local law or sector rules; they provide a framework for translating broad responsibility into operational controls.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a governance checklist for one AI use case, naming the owner, purpose, affected groups, risk controls, review process, and escalation route."
Key point:
- Responsible AI requires lifecycle governance rather than a one-time approval.
- Human oversight, transparency, safety, and accountability should be built into operations.
Source links:
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- OECD: AI Principles
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
52. Q: How can the NIST AI Risk Management Framework support the chapter's call for thoughtful engagement?
A: Show answer
The NIST AI Risk Management Framework organizes risk work around four functions: Govern, Map, Measure, and Manage. Governance establishes policies, roles, and accountability. Mapping identifies the context, intended use, affected people, and potential harms. Measuring evaluates performance and risks using appropriate methods and evidence. Managing prioritizes responses, monitoring, and improvement. This structure supports the chapter's argument because it treats AI use as a continuing socio-technical process rather than a simple tool purchase. The framework is voluntary and must be adapted to the organization's context; it is not a universal legal compliance certificate. It can nevertheless help teams convert general concern into documented responsibilities and practical action.
Study instruction:
- "Apply Govern, Map, Measure, and Manage to a proposed AI assistant and identify one concrete deliverable for each function."
Key point:
- Risk management should continue across design, deployment, use, and evaluation.
- A framework structures judgment but does not remove the need for context-specific decisions.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- NIST: Generative Artificial Intelligence Profile
53. Q: What role should human oversight play when AI supports a consequential decision?
A: Show answer
Human oversight means more than placing a person at the end of an automated process to approve a recommendation. The reviewer needs adequate information, time, competence, authority, and a genuine ability to question or reject the output. Responsibilities should be clear, and affected people should have a route to explanation or correction where appropriate. The level of oversight should reflect the potential harm and the context. UNESCO states that AI should not displace ultimate human responsibility and accountability. The chapter's concern about cognitive delegation makes this practical: a human who cannot explain or challenge the output is not providing meaningful oversight.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a decision workflow and test whether the human reviewer has information, competence, time, authority, and an effective override."
Key point:
- Human review is meaningful only when the reviewer can understand and challenge the system.
- Accountability remains with responsible people and organizations.
Source links:
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- OECD: AI Principles
54. Q: How can organizations manage risks from synthetic content and impersonation?
A: Show answer
Organizations should establish trusted channels for important instructions, verify identity independently for payments and sensitive requests, and preserve records of original communications. Public-facing content can use provenance and disclosure practices where appropriate, while staff should be trained not to rely solely on visual or audio realism. Security teams should plan for impersonation, manipulated documents, and false evidence in incident response exercises. Technical methods such as watermarking, metadata, content credentials, and detection can help, but they are not foolproof and should be combined with process controls and corroboration. NIST's synthetic-content report provides a useful overview of transparency and provenance approaches.
Study instruction:
- "Run a tabletop exercise involving a cloned executive voice requesting an urgent transfer and document detection, verification, escalation, and recovery steps."
Key point:
- Technical detection should be combined with independent identity and transaction controls.
- Provenance improves transparency but cannot guarantee truth or authenticity in every case.
Source links:
- NIST: Reducing Risks Posed by Synthetic Content
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
55. Q: How should an organization respond when AI-generated content may infringe intellectual-property rights?
A: Show answer
Pause publication or distribution until the source and rights position have been reviewed. Identify the prompt, input materials, tool and model used, provider terms, output, and intended use. Compare the output with potentially relevant protected works and assess whether permissions, licenses, attribution, or legal review are required. Preserve records so the organization can explain how the material was created and reviewed. If a concern is substantiated, revise, replace, license, or withdraw the material as appropriate. The response should follow applicable law and contractual terms; it should not assume that a model's generation of an output automatically clears the rights of the user or provider.
Study instruction:
- "Create an incident checklist for a disputed AI-generated image or text, including preservation of records and publication controls."
Key point:
- Publication requires rights review appropriate to the material and jurisdiction.
- Documentation supports investigation, correction, and defensible decisions.
Source links:
- WIPO: Generative AI and Intellectual Property
56. Q: How can organizations make AI adoption fairer for workers affected by automation?
A: Show answer
They should assess tasks and job quality before restructuring roles, involve workers in identifying risks and opportunities, provide training before and during transitions, and communicate how decisions will be made. Managers should distinguish productivity improvements from reductions in headcount and consider whether AI can remove repetitive work while allowing staff to focus on higher-value responsibilities. Where displacement is likely, organizations should plan redeployment, reskilling, consultation, and appropriate support. The ILO's 2025 analysis emphasizes that transformation is more likely than full replacement for many exposed jobs, but exposure can still create major adjustment needs. Fairness requires attention to who gains, who bears the cost, and who participates in decisions.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a worker-impact assessment for one proposed AI deployment, including tasks, affected roles, training, consultation, and transition support."
Key point:
- Worker participation and training should accompany technology deployment.
- Task transformation can be disruptive even when whole jobs are not eliminated.
Source links:
- ILO: Generative AI and Jobs, 2025
- OECD: AI Principles
57. Q: Why is it important to consider who benefits and who bears the costs of AI?
A: Show answer
A technology can increase aggregate productivity while concentrating profits, shifting environmental burdens, or imposing transition costs on particular workers and communities. The chapter's positive-sum argument does not remove this distributional question. For example, a data centre may provide services to distant customers while placing new demands on a local grid or water system. An organization may gain efficiency while employees face new monitoring or job insecurity. A fair assessment therefore identifies affected groups, the distribution of benefits and burdens, the ability to participate in decisions, and available remedies. This approach connects AI strategy to environmental justice, labor policy, human rights, and accountable governance.
Study instruction:
- "Map benefits and burdens for users, workers, creators, local communities, suppliers, and the environment in one AI project."
Key point:
- Aggregate gains can coexist with concentrated costs.
- Responsible adoption examines distribution, participation, and remedies as well as efficiency.
Source links:
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- OECD: AI Principles
- ILO: Generative AI and Jobs, 2025
58. Q: What is the difference between thoughtful engagement and simply chasing every new AI tool?
A: Show answer
Thoughtful engagement starts with a real problem and asks whether a particular capability can improve it safely and measurably. It considers the tool's limitations, data practices, costs, and effect on human work. Chasing tools begins with novelty and can lead to frequent switching, fragmented workflows, duplicated effort, and weak governance. The chapter does not advocate adopting every new product. It advocates learning enough to understand the changing environment and participating in decisions about useful applications. A disciplined organization can test a small number of tools, compare results, retain only those that meet standards, and stop using those that create more risk than value.
Study instruction:
- "Select one actual workflow and compare two AI tools and a non-AI alternative against the same criteria before recommending adoption."
Key point:
- Problem-led evaluation is more useful than novelty-led adoption.
- Selective testing and clear exit criteria reduce tool sprawl and unmanaged risk.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- OECD: AI Principles
59. Q: How can AI be used without making the user dependent on it for every stage of a task?
A: Show answer
The user can preserve independent work at key stages. Before asking AI, define the question and record an initial hypothesis. During the interaction, use the system to identify gaps, challenge assumptions, compare alternatives, and locate sources rather than simply generate a final answer. Afterward, verify important claims, write a synthesis independently, and decide what should be retained in the user's own knowledge system. Some routine tasks can be delegated, but the user should understand what was delegated and how its quality was checked. The chapter's emphasis on thoughtful engagement is a habit of active collaboration, not a requirement to use AI at every step.
Study instruction:
- "Design a workflow that deliberately reserves problem framing, critical evaluation, and final decision-making for the human user."
Key point:
- Dependency is reduced when users retain meaningful cognitive work.
- Delegation should be selective, transparent, and followed by verification.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
60. Q: What warning signs indicate that AI use is weakening rather than improving a person's understanding?
A: Show answer
Warning signs include being unable to explain an output without reopening it, accepting claims that cannot be sourced, losing track of the original problem, using summaries without reading critical evidence, and feeling productive while repeatedly postponing judgment. Another sign is that the person becomes less willing to attempt a task independently or cannot recognize when an answer conflicts with domain knowledge. These signs do not prove lasting cognitive decline, but they indicate that the workflow may be bypassing learning. A corrective response is to restore independent attempts, ask the system to explain and challenge its reasoning, verify with primary material, and review whether the user can transfer the knowledge to a new case.
Study instruction:
- "Use a weekly self-check to test independent recall, source verification, error detection, and ability to apply AI-assisted learning to a new problem."
Key point:
- Dependence can be visible in reduced ability to explain, verify, or transfer knowledge.
- The response is to redesign the learning process, not to assume all AI use is harmful.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
61. Q: How can a manager measure whether AI is improving work rather than merely increasing output volume?
A: Show answer
Measure the outcome that matters to the process: accuracy, completeness, cycle time after review, error correction, customer or beneficiary impact, accessibility, staff workload, and risk events. Compare results with a baseline and include the time required for verification, rework, and governance. Track whether staff understand the output and whether important decisions remain accountable. Increased volume may be valuable in some settings, but it can also mean more low-quality material or hidden review burden. The chapter's distinction between thoughtful engagement and raw adoption suggests that organizations should evaluate quality and learning as well as throughput.
Study instruction:
- "Build a pilot scorecard with baseline measures, output quality, review effort, user learning, risk indicators, and stop-or-scale criteria."
Key point:
- Productivity should be measured net of review, rework, and harm.
- Quality and human capability are important outcomes alongside speed and volume.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- OECD: AI Principles
62. Q: What should be included in a scenario plan for the future effects of AI?
A: Show answer
A scenario plan should identify the technology or capability being considered, the constraint it may remove, the stakeholders affected, and the first-order change. It should then explore possible second- and third-order effects, including new opportunities, dependencies, misuse, market concentration, environmental pressure, and changes to work. Develop more than one plausible pathway, including a high-benefit case, a harmful or unequal case, and a mixed case. State assumptions, early indicators, uncertainties, and decisions that can be adjusted as evidence emerges. The chapter's Budding Effect is useful for generating scenarios, while risk frameworks help identify controls and monitoring needs.
Study instruction:
- "Create three scenarios for one AI capability and list the assumptions, indicators, affected groups, and decisions associated with each."
Key point:
- Scenario planning explores conditional futures rather than claiming certainty.
- Monitoring indicators allows organizations to adapt as second-order effects emerge.
Source links:
- NIST: Artificial Intelligence Risk Management Framework
- International Energy Agency: Energy and AI
63. Q: How should readers balance the chapter's optimism with its warning that AI harms remain real?
A: Show answer
The chapter's optimism is best understood as confidence in human capacity to influence change, not confidence that the technology will automatically produce good outcomes. Readers should take environmental impacts, rights disputes, synthetic media, cognitive dependence, and labor disruption seriously while also recognizing opportunities to improve access, research, productivity, and problem-solving. A balanced response sets boundaries for unacceptable uses, tests beneficial applications, protects affected groups, and participates in governance. It rejects both complacency and paralysis. The author does not ask readers to stop advocating for regulation or ethical implementation; he argues that such work should occur alongside informed engagement rather than in place of it.
Study instruction:
- "Write a balanced position statement that names three serious risks, three potential benefits, and the actions needed to influence outcomes responsibly."
Key point:
- Optimism is conditional on responsible action and governance.
- Engagement should strengthen, not replace, efforts to reduce harm.
Source links:
- UNESCO: Recommendation on the Ethics of Artificial Intelligence
- OECD: AI Principles
64. Q: What are the most important lessons to remember from Chapter 3?
A: Show answer
Chapter 3 presents AI as a major transition that cannot be understood through either fear or enthusiasm alone. It identifies environmental, intellectual-property, information-integrity, cognitive, and employment risks. It argues that ignoring AI does not stop change, while careless delegation can weaken understanding and judgment. Historical transitions show that disruption and opportunity can coexist, but positive outcomes require deliberate human effort and fair institutions. The Budding Effect highlights how removing a constraint may create consequences far beyond the original task, although those effects remain uncertain. The chapter's practical recommendation is to engage with curiosity, patience, context, verification, and human accountability while working to shape responsible systems and distribute benefits more fairly.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a chapter review that explains the risks, two dead ends, historical analogies, first- and second-order effects, and the case for thoughtful engagement."
Key point:
- Neither opting out nor uncritical delegation is a sufficient response to AI.
- The future depends on human judgment, governance, and how society manages indirect effects.
